Neither method is universally appropriate.

45. When Direct Sowing Is Particularly Suitable
Direct sowing may be appropriate when:
- the crop germinates reliably in field conditions,
- soil moisture can be managed,
- temperature is suitable,
- seedling emergence is strong,
- weeds can be controlled,
- field conditions are suitable,
- mechanized sowing is desired,
- transplanting would provide little advantage.

46. When Transplanting May Be Preferred
Transplanting can be useful when:
- early growth requires greater environmental protection,

- seedlings are easier to manage in a nursery,
- the crop benefits from a controlled nursery stage,
- very small seeds are difficult to establish directly under field conditions,
- field preparation needs to be completed before crop establishment,
- the crop has an established transplanting production system.

47. Direct Sowing Workflow
A practical workflow is:
1. Crop Selection 
↓
2. Select Suitable Seed 
↓
3. Determine Sowing Time 
↓
4. Prepare/Assess Soil 
↓
5. Determine Seed Rate 
↓
6. Determine Row and Plant Spacing 
↓
7. Set Sowing Depth 
↓
8. Place Seed 
↓
9. Ensure Seed−Soil Contact 
↓
10. Manage Moisture 
↓
11. Monitor Emergence 

↓
12. Manage Weeds/Pests/Diseases 
↓
13. Maintain Desired Plant Population 

48. Direct Sowing Quality Check
After sowing, inspect:

Seed depth
Is it uniform?

Seed spacing
Are skips/doubles occurring?

Seed-soil contact
Is the seed properly covered?

Moisture
Is adequate moisture available?

Emergence
Are seedlings emerging uniformly?

Plant population
Is the achieved population close to the target?

49. Diagnosing Poor Direct-Sown Crop
Establishment
If emergence is poor, investigate systematically:
Seed 
↓
Placement 
↓
Soil 

↓
Weather 
↓
Pests/Diseases 

Seed problems
- low viability,
- poor germination,
- low vigor,
- poor health.

Placement problems
- too deep,
- too shallow,
- skips,
- doubles,
- poor soil contact.

Soil problems
- drought,
- waterlogging,
- compaction,
- crusting,
- excessive clods.

Environmental problems
- heat,
- cold,
- excessive rain,
- drought.

Biological problems
- insects,
- pathogens,

- rodents,
- birds.

50. Direct Sowing and Crop Establishment
The complete relationship is:
Direct Sowing→Seed Placement→Germination→Emergence→Establishment→Plant Population d          
A successful direct-sowing operation therefore aims not merely to put seed into the soil, but to create
conditions for a uniform, healthy crop stand.

51. Important Formulas
Plant population
Plants/ha=10,000Row spacing(m)×Plant spacing(m)   Row\
spacing(m) Plant\ spacing(m)   

Emergence percentage
Emergence%=Emerged seedlingsSeeds planted×100 
 Seeds\ planted  100  

Establishment percentage
Establishment%=Established plantsExpected plant positions×100   Expected\ plant\ positions  100  

Seed rate concept
Seed Rate≈Desired Plant PopulationSeeds/kg×Germination×Purity×Establishment   Seeds/kg Germination Purity Establishment   
Percentages should be expressed as fractions in the calculation.

52. Common Misconceptions

❌ Direct sowing means broadcasting.
No.

Broadcasting is only one method of direct sowing.

❌ Direct sowing means no soil preparation.
No.
It can be used with conventional tillage, minimum tillage or zero tillage.

❌ Direct sowing means no machinery.
No.

Seed drills and precision planters are important direct-sowing equipment.

❌ Direct sowing always gives lower yield than transplanting.
❌ Direct sowing means planting any seed directly into soil.
❌ Direct sowing is the same as direct planting.

Not universally. Yield depends on crop, cultivar, environment and management.

Successful direct sowing requires appropriate seed quality, depth, spacing, moisture and soil conditions.

Not always. "Direct sowing" normally emphasizes seed; "direct planting" can also refer to vegetative
planting material.

53. Key Distinctions for Module 10
Concept
Core question
Sowing time
When should the seed be sown?
Planting time
When should the planting operation occur?
Seed rate
How much seed is required?
Plant population How many plants should occupy the area?
Row spacing
How far apart should rows be?
Plant spacing
How far apart should plants be?
Sowing depth
How deep should the seed be?
Seed placement Where and how should the seed be positioned?
Direct sowing
Should the seed establish directly in the final field?
Transplanting
Should a nursery-raised seedling be moved to the field?

54. Final Conceptual Model
Direct sowing is not simply:
Put Seed in Soil 
It is:

Right Time+Right Seed+Right Rate+Right Spacing+Right Depth+Good Soil Contact+Suitable Moistur
e+Suitable Temperature  +   +   +
  +   +   +   +
   
↓
Uniform Germination   
↓
Uniform Emergence   
↓
Desired Plant Population   
↓
Strong Crop Establishment   
↓
Growth and Yield Potential   

55. Exam-Ready Definition
Direct sowing is a crop-establishment method in which seeds are placed directly into
the final field where the crop is intended to grow, at an appropriate time, depth and
spacing, without first raising and transplanting nursery seedlings. Its success depends
- n seed quality, soil moisture and structure, temperature, seed placement, seed-soil
contact, and subsequent crop management.

One-line memory
Direct Sowing = Seed → Directly into Final Field → Germination → Establishment  
And the key distinction:
Direct Sowing = No Nursery Transplanting  

## Module 10 — Sowing & Planting
## 9. Transplanting
## 1. Introduction
Transplanting is a crop-establishment method in which a young plant or seedling is first raised in a
nursery or protected environment and then moved to the final field where it will continue
growing.
In simple terms:
Seed→Nursery→Seedling→Field→Established Crop       
It is therefore different from direct sowing, where the seed is placed directly into the final field.

## 2. Definition
Transplanting is the agricultural practice of removing or transferring a nurseryraised seedling or young plant from its original growing medium or nursery to its final
production field at a suitable stage and spacing.
The fundamental idea is:
Grow the young plant first → move it → establish it in the field  

3. Basic Transplanting Process
The general sequence is:
Seed Selection 
↓
Nursery Preparation 
↓
Seed Sowing 
↓
Germination 
↓

Seedling Growth 
↓
Seedling Selection 
↓
Hardening 
↓
Field Preparation 
↓
Transplanting 
↓
Watering/Establishment 
↓
Root Recovery 
↓
Vegetative Growth 

4. Transplanting vs Direct Sowing
This is the most important distinction.
Direct sowing
Transplanting
Seed goes directly into final field
Seedling goes into final field
Germination occurs in field
Germination usually occurs in nursery
No transplantation
Requires transplantation
No transplant shock
Transplant shock can occur
Field conditions affect seed immediately Nursery provides early-stage control
Common for many field crops
Common for many vegetables and selected field crops

Memory
Direct Sowing: Seed → Field  
Transplanting: Seed → Nursery → Seedling → Field  

5. Why Transplanting Is Used
Transplanting allows the farmer to separate early seedling production from final field production.

It can provide:
- better control of early growth,
- efficient nursery management,
- selection of healthy seedlings,
- uniform plant establishment,
- efficient use of seed,
- better use of field preparation time,
- earlier crop establishment in some production systems,
- protection of young plants during vulnerable stages.

6. Main Principle of Transplanting
The fundamental principle is:
Produce a Healthy Seedling→Move it Carefully→Minimize Root Damage→Rapidly Re-establish Root
s      
The biggest challenge is that transplanting physically changes the plant's environment and may disturb
its root system.

7. Seedling Used for Transplanting
A transplantable seedling should generally have:
- healthy roots,
- healthy leaves,
- sturdy stem,
- adequate vigor,
- appropriate age,
- appropriate size,
- freedom from serious pests/diseases,
- sufficient root development.
A good seedling is not necessarily the largest seedling.
Good Transplant≠Largest Transplant    

The objective is an appropriately developed, vigorous and field-ready plant.

8. Nursery Stage
Before transplanting, seedlings are generally produced in a nursery.
A nursery may be:

Open nursery
Seedlings are raised in field beds or open conditions.

Protected nursery
Seedlings are raised under:
- shade structures,
- net houses,
- greenhouses,
- polyhouses,
- other controlled/protected environments.

Container nursery
Seedlings are raised in:
- trays,
- pots,
- cells,
- bags,
- other containers.

9. Why Raise Seedlings in a Nursery?
The nursery provides an opportunity to manage:
- water,
- temperature,
- light,
- nutrients,
- pests,

- diseases,
- spacing,
- growing medium.
Therefore:
Nursery→Controlled Early Growth→Better Seedling Preparation     

10. Seedling Quality
Seedling quality can be considered through:
Seedling Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health  =
  +   +   +    
Important characteristics include:

Genetic
- true-to-type,
- correct variety/hybrid.

Physical
- undamaged,
- properly developed,
- good root system.

Physiological
- vigorous,
- appropriate age,
- suitable size.

Health
- free from significant pest/disease problems.

11. Transplanting Age
There is no universal transplanting age for all crops.
It depends on:

- crop,
- variety/hybrid,
- seedling growth rate,
- nursery conditions,
- root development,
- season,
- transplanting method.
The correct stage is generally when the seedling is strong enough to survive handling but young
enough to establish rapidly.

12. Why Over-Aged Seedlings Can Be
Problematic
An over-aged seedling may develop:
- excessive root binding,
- excessive stem elongation,
- transplant shock,
- slower establishment,
- reduced recovery.
Therefore:
Older Seedling≠Automatically Better Seedling    

13. Why Very Young Seedlings Can Be
Problematic
Very young seedlings may have:
- weak roots,
- small shoots,
- limited stored reserves,
- poor resistance to field conditions,

- greater handling damage.
Therefore the seedling must reach an appropriate physiological stage before transplanting.

## 14. Hardening
Hardening is the gradual acclimatization of nursery seedlings to the conditions they will experience
after transplanting.
Nursery:
Protected/Managed Conditions 
Field:
Higher Environmental Stress 
Hardening helps reduce this transition shock.
It can involve controlled adjustment of:
- watering,
- shade,
- humidity,
- temperature exposure,
- ventilation.
The exact method depends on crop and nursery system.

15. Transplant Shock
One of the most important concepts.
Transplant shock is the temporary physiological stress experienced by a plant after being moved from
its nursery/container environment to the field.
Possible causes include:
- root damage,
- root loss,
- water stress,
- high temperature,
- high radiation,

- wind,
- low humidity,
- poor soil contact,
- sudden environmental change.

16. Mechanism of Transplant Shock
A simplified sequence is:
Root Damage→Water Uptake↓→Plant Water Stress→Wilting→Growth Reduction 
At the same time:
Leaf Water Loss>Root Water Uptake  >  
can temporarily occur.

17. Root Damage During Transplanting
Roots can be damaged during:
- lifting,
- pulling,
- transportation,
- separation from nursery beds,
- handling,
- planting.
Root damage can reduce:
- water uptake,
- nutrient uptake,
- anchorage,
- establishment speed.
Therefore:
Gentle Root Handling→Better Recovery Potential    

18. Root-to-Shoot Ratio
A useful concept is:
R:S=Root BiomassShoot Biomass  Shoot\ Biomass   
A well-developed root system helps the plant supply water to the shoot after transplanting.
A very large shoot with a relatively weak root system can increase water stress after transplanting.

19. Transplanting Depth
Seedling depth is different from seed sowing depth.
The seedling should generally be planted at a depth appropriate for its root system and stem/crown
characteristics.
Too shallow:
- roots may be exposed,
- poor anchorage,
- drying.
Too deep:
- stem/crown may be buried excessively,
- oxygen conditions around sensitive tissues can become unsuitable,
- growth may be impaired.
Therefore:
Correct Transplanting Depth=Adequate Root Coverage+Proper Shoot Position  =   +    
Crop-specific recommendations are important.

20. Transplanting Spacing
Like direct sowing, transplanting requires appropriate:
- row spacing,
- plant spacing,
- plant population.

For rectangular spacing:
Plants/ha=10,000Row Spacing(m)×Plant Spacing(m)   Row\
Spacing(m) Plant\ Spacing(m)   
For example:
0.5m×0.2m0.5m 0.2m
gives:
10,0000.5×0.2=100,000 plants/ha  0.5 0.2  = 100,000\ plants/ha
under ideal spacing.

21. Transplanting and Plant Population
Unlike direct sowing, the farmer can often inspect and select seedlings before planting.
This provides an opportunity to establish a more uniform stand.
However, actual population can still be reduced by:
- transplant mortality,
- pests,
- diseases,
- drought,
- waterlogging,
- poor planting,
- mechanical damage.
Therefore:
Target Population≠Final Population 

22. Transplanting and Soil Moisture
Adequate soil moisture is extremely important after transplanting.
The newly transplanted plant has a disturbed root system.
Therefore:
Root Uptake↓+High Transpiration→Water Stress 
Maintaining appropriate soil moisture during establishment helps recovery.

But excessive irrigation can cause:
- waterlogging,
- oxygen deficiency,
- root disease.

23. Transplanting and Irrigation
Immediately after transplanting, irrigation may be important to improve:
- root-soil contact,
- water availability,
- turgor,
- establishment.
The goal is not simply maximum water.
Adequate Moisture≠Continuous Saturation    

24. Transplanting and Soil Contact
The roots should have close contact with the surrounding soil.
Poor root-soil contact can create air gaps.
Therefore:
Root−Soil Contact→Water Transfer+Root Establishment 
Proper planting and soil firming around the root zone can help, depending on the system.

25. Transplanting and Soil Aeration
Roots require oxygen for respiration.
Organic Substrate+O2→CO2+H2O+Energy +O 2  CO 2+H 2O+
 
Waterlogged soil can reduce oxygen availability.
Thus transplanting into excessively wet soil can cause:
- root stress,

- poor growth,
- disease risk.

26. Transplanting and Temperature
After transplanting, seedlings are exposed to field temperatures.
Extreme:
- heat,
- cold,
- frost,
can increase transplant stress.
Young seedlings are particularly sensitive to sudden environmental changes.

27. Transplanting and Humidity
High humidity can reduce atmospheric water demand, while low humidity can increase it.
The relevant concept is vapor pressure deficit:
VPD=es−ea 
Higher VPD generally increases atmospheric drying demand.
Therefore transplanting under high evaporative demand can increase water stress.

28. Transplanting and Wind
Wind can increase water loss from leaves.
Wind→Boundary Layer Reduction→Water Vapor Transfer↑→Transplant Water Stress↑ 
Wind can also physically damage weak seedlings.

29. Transplanting and Shade
Temporary shading can sometimes help newly transplanted seedlings by reducing:
- radiation load,

- leaf temperature,
- transpiration.
But excessive shading can reduce:
- photosynthesis,
- growth,
- hardening.
Therefore shade management must be appropriate to crop and conditions.

30. Transplanting and Root Establishment
After planting:
Transplant→RootRecovery→NewRootGrowth→Water/
NutrientUptake→ShootRecovery 
Successful transplanting is therefore not complete when the seedling is placed in the soil.
Establishment occurs when the plant resumes stable growth and root function.

31. Transplanting and Nursery Seedlings
This connects directly with your previous topic:
Seed→Nursery→Nursery Seedling→Transplanting→Field Crop 
A nursery seedling is therefore the planting material, while transplanting is the operation/process
used to move it to the field.

32. Methods of Transplanting
A. Manual transplanting
Workers manually:
1. make/open planting position,
2. place seedling,
3. position roots,

4. cover roots with soil,
5. firm soil around plant,
6. irrigate as needed.
Common in small-scale and labor-intensive systems.

33. Mechanical Transplanting
Mechanical transplanters can:
- pick seedlings,
- meter seedlings,
- place seedlings,
- maintain row spacing,
- control planting geometry.
Advantages:
- faster,
- reduced manual labor,
- more uniform spacing under suitable conditions.
Limitations:
- machinery cost,
- seedling specifications,
- field preparation requirements,
- operator skill,
- maintenance.

34. Rice Transplanting
Rice provides a classic example.
The general system can be:
Rice Seed→Nursery→Rice Seedlings→Transplanting→Rice Field 
Transplanting may be:

- manual,
- mechanical.
Modern rice systems can also use direct-seeded rice, so transplanting is a method rather than an
unavoidable requirement.

35. Vegetable Transplanting
Many vegetables are commonly raised as seedlings and later transplanted.
Examples include:
- tomato,
- chilli,
- brinjal/eggplant,
- cabbage,
- cauliflower,
- some cucurbits,
- many high-value vegetables.
The exact suitability depends on crop and production system.

36. Transplanting in Protected Cultivation
Seedlings can be produced in:
- plug trays,
- protected nurseries,
- greenhouses.
Then they are transplanted into:
- soil beds,
- raised beds,
- containers,
- hydroponic systems.
Thus:
Nursery Production+Transplanting 

can be part of protected cultivation.

37. Transplanting and Hydroponics
Hydroponic systems can also use transplanted seedlings.
For example:
Seed→Nursery/Plug→Seedling→Hydroponic System     Therefore:
Hydroponics≠Transplanting 
Hydroponics is a production/growing system; transplanting is an establishment operation.

38. Bare-Root vs Container-Grown Seedlings
Bare-root seedlings
Roots are removed from the nursery medium with little or no attached growing medium.
Advantages:
- lighter,
- potentially cheaper to transport.
Disadvantages:
- greater root exposure,
- greater root disturbance,
- greater risk of drying.

Container-grown seedlings
Roots remain in a plug/root ball.
Advantages:
- less root disturbance,
- easier handling,
- potentially faster establishment.
Possible problem:
- root binding if kept too long.

39. Root Binding
Root binding occurs when roots become densely concentrated and often circle around the inside of a
container.
This can restrict normal outward root growth after planting.
Long Container Stay→Root Binding Risk↑ 
Proper nursery management and timely transplanting reduce this risk.

40. Transplanting and Seedling Hardening
A well-hardened seedling is better prepared for:
- wind,
- sunlight,
- lower humidity,
- field temperature fluctuations.
Therefore:
Hardening→Acclimatization→Better Field Transition 
Hardening does not make plants immune to transplant shock.

41. Transplanting and Seedling Selection
Before transplanting, seedlings may be sorted.
Remove or avoid seedlings that are:
- severely diseased,
- damaged,
- extremely weak,
- badly malformed,
- severely root-bound,
- clearly off-type.

This helps improve stand uniformity.

42. Transplanting and Genetic Purity
If the crop is a hybrid or specific variety, correct seedlings must be planted.
Therefore:
Correct Seed→Correct Seedling→Correct Field Variety 
Transplanting does not change the genetic identity of the seedling.

43. Transplanting and Planting Material
Different planting materials can be transplanted.
Examples include:
- nursery seedlings,
- rooted cuttings,
- grafted plants,
- container-grown plants.
However, in conventional crop-production terminology, transplanting most commonly refers to
moving seedlings or young plants.

44. Transplanting Grafted Seedlings
A grafted plant may be transplanted after the graft union has sufficiently developed.
The process may be:
Rootstock+Scion→Grafted Plant→Nursery Growth→Transplanting 
This is common in some horticultural systems.

45. Transplanting and Fertilizer
Nutrients may be supplied before or after transplanting depending on the crop and production system.
Care is needed because concentrated fertilizer placed too close to young roots can cause injury.

Thus:
Nutrient Management+Root Protection 
are both important.

46. Transplanting and Weed Management
A transplanted seedling may initially have a size advantage over newly emerging weeds.
However, weeds can still compete for:
- water,
- nutrients,
- light,
- space.
Therefore transplanting does not eliminate weed management.

47. Transplanting and Crop Establishment
A successful transplant operation produces:
- upright seedlings,
- good root-soil contact,
- correct depth,
- correct spacing,
- adequate moisture,
- minimum root damage.
The sequence is:
Transplanting→RootRecovery→NewRootGrowth→Water/
NutrientUptake→ActiveGrowth 

48. Advantages of Transplanting
1. Better early-stage control
Seedlings can be managed in a nursery.

2. Seedling selection
Healthy and uniform seedlings can be selected.

3. Efficient seed use
Especially important for expensive hybrid seed.

4. No field germination risk
The seed has already germinated before field planting.

5. Field preparation flexibility
The field can be prepared while seedlings are developing in the nursery.

6. Potentially uniform crop stand
Uniform seedlings can be planted at controlled spacing.

7. Suitable for certain crops
Some crops perform well with nursery-based establishment.

49. Disadvantages of Transplanting
1. Transplant shock
Root disturbance and environmental change can temporarily reduce growth.

2. Nursery cost
Requires:
- nursery space,
- labor,
- water,
- materials.

3. Additional operation
Seedlings must be:
- lifted,
- transported,

- planted.

4. Root damage
Improper handling can damage roots.

5. Labor requirement
Manual transplanting can be labor-intensive.

6. Machinery requirement
Mechanical transplanting requires specialized equipment.

7. Seedling quality dependence
Poor seedlings produce poor field establishment.

50. Direct Sowing vs Transplanting — Complete

51. When Transplanting Is Useful
Transplanting may be particularly useful when:
- early seedling growth benefits from nursery protection,
- seed is expensive,
- uniform seedlings are desirable,
- field preparation takes time,
- the crop is commonly transplanted,
- field establishment from seed is difficult under the prevailing conditions,
- labor or transplanting machinery is available.

52. When Direct Sowing May Be More
Appropriate
Direct sowing may be preferable when:
- the crop establishes reliably from seed,
- field conditions are suitable,
- transplanting offers little advantage,
- nursery production would add unnecessary cost,
- mechanized direct seeding is available.

53. Transplanting Workflow
A complete practical workflow is:
1. Select Quality Seed 
↓
2. Prepare Nursery 
↓
3. Sow Seed 
↓
4. Manage Germination 

↓
5. Raise Healthy Seedlings 
↓
6. Harden Seedlings 
↓
7. Prepare Field 
↓
8. Select Suitable Seedlings 
↓
9. Transport Carefully 
↓
10. Transplant at Correct Depth and Spacing 
↓
11. Ensure Root−Soil Contact 
↓
12. Irrigate/Manage Moisture 
↓
13. Monitor Transplant Shock 
↓
14. Manage Weeds, Pests, Diseases and Nutrition 
↓
15. Confirm Establishment 

54. Transplanting Quality Assessment
After transplanting, inspect:

Planting depth
Are seedlings planted uniformly?

Spacing
Are plants at the intended positions?

Root condition
Were roots damaged?

Soil contact
Are roots properly surrounded by soil?

Plant orientation
Are plants upright?

Moisture
Is the root zone adequately moist?

Survival
How many plants survived?

Recovery
Are new leaves/root growth appearing?

55. Transplant Survival Percentage
A simple measure is:
Transplant Survival%=Number of surviving transplantsNumber of transplants planted×100   Number\ of\ transplants\ planted 
 100  
For example, if 9,500 seedlings are transplanted and 9,025 survive:
90259500×100=95%  9500  100 = 95\%

56. Transplant Mortality
Transplant mortality is:
Mortality%=100−Survival% 
Using the previous example:
100−95=5%100-95=5\%

57. Transplant Shock Indicators
Possible indicators include:

- temporary wilting,
- leaf drooping,
- reduced photosynthesis,
- reduced growth,
- leaf yellowing under severe stress,
- delayed new leaf development,
- reduced root growth.
Recovery is indicated by:
- restored turgor,
- new root growth,
- new leaves,
- resumed shoot growth.

58. Factors Affecting Transplanting Success
Success=f(Seedling Quality,Age,Root Condition,Hardening,Soil,Moisture,Temperature,Humidity,Wind
,Depth,Spacing,Handling,Aftercare) 
This is why transplanting is more than simply putting a seedling into a hole.

59. Common Transplanting Mistakes
1. Using weak seedlings
Weak seedlings have lower establishment potential.

2. Using over-aged seedlings
Can lead to root binding and slower recovery.

3. Damaging roots
Reduces water and nutrient uptake.

4. Planting too deep
Can impair the root/crown environment.

5. Planting too shallow
Can expose roots and reduce anchorage.

6. Poor spacing
Creates uneven competition.

7. Transplanting into very dry soil
Causes water stress.

8. Transplanting into waterlogged soil
Causes oxygen stress.

9. Transplanting during extreme heat
Increases water loss.

10. Poor hardening
Makes environmental transition more difficult.

60. Common Misconceptions

❌ "Transplanting always produces higher yield."
❌ "The bigger the seedling, the better."
❌ "Transplanting eliminates germination problems."
❌ "Transplanting means there is no root damage."
❌ "Transplanting and nursery raising are the same thing."

Not necessarily. Yield depends on crop, cultivar, environment and management.

Not necessarily. Over-aged seedlings can establish poorly.

It moves the germination stage to the nursery, but field establishment problems can still occur.

Root damage is possible and is one of the major risks.

No.
Nursery Raising=Producing the Seedling   
Transplanting=Moving the Seedling to the Final Field   

❌ "All crops should be transplanted."
No. Crop biology and production system determine the appropriate establishment method.

Direct sowing
Seed placed directly in final field
Planting depth
Vertical position of seedling/plant
Plant spacing
Distance between plants
Plant population Number of plants per unit area

Successful recovery and continued growth after planting

63. Direct Sowing vs Transplanting —
Conceptual Decision
The fundamental decision is:

Route A — Direct establishment
Can the crop reliably germinate and establish directly in the field?   
If yes, direct sowing may be suitable.

Route B — Nursery establishment
Would controlled seedling production provide an advantage?   
If yes, transplanting may be suitable.
The decision depends on:
Crop+Climate+Soil+Seed+Labor+Water+Machinery+Economics 

64. Complete Transplanting Model
The whole concept can be summarized:
Quality Seed   
↓

Healthy Nursery Seedling   
↓

↓

↓
Good Root-Soil Contact   
↓
Adequate Moisture + Suitable Environment   
↓
Root Recovery   
↓
Plant Establishment   
↓
Vegetative Growth   
↓
Flowering → Yield

66. Exam-Ready Definition
Transplanting is a crop-establishment practice in which nursery-raised seedlings or
young plants are carefully transferred to their final growing location at an
appropriate stage, depth and spacing. Successful transplanting requires healthy and
hardened seedlings, minimal root damage, proper root-soil contact, adequate
moisture, suitable environmental conditions and appropriate post-transplant
management.

67. One-Line Memory
Transplanting = Raise → Harden → Move → Plant → Recover → Establish   

Most important distinction:
Direct Sowing = Seed → Final Field  
Transplanting = Seed → Nursery → Seedling → Final Field  
And the key biological challenge:
Transplanting Success→Minimize Root/Water Stress→Rapid Root Recovery→Successful Establishme
nt      

## Module 10 — Sowing & Planting
Nursery Raising
## 1. Introduction
Nursery raising is the process of producing healthy, vigorous and uniform young plants under
managed conditions before they are transferred to their final growing location.
In a nursery, the farmer provides favorable conditions for the germination, early root development
and shoot growth of seedlings.
The basic sequence is:

Quality Seed→Nursery→Germination→Seedling Growth→Hardening→Transplanting→Field Establis
hment         
Nursery raising is therefore an early crop-establishment stage, while transplanting is the subsequent
- peration of moving the nursery-grown plant to the final field.

## 2. Definition
Nursery raising is the systematic production and management of young crop plants
under controlled or semi-controlled conditions until they reach a suitable stage for
transplanting or further cultivation.

In simple words
Nursery raising = Producing a healthy plant before putting it in the final field  

3. Why Is Nursery Raising Required?
The main purpose is to provide young plants with favorable conditions during their most vulnerable
stage.
A nursery allows better management of:
- seed germination,
- moisture,
- temperature,
- light,
- nutrition,
- spacing,
- weeds,
- pests,
- diseases,
- growing medium,
- seedling growth.
Thus:

Nursery→Better Early-Stage Management→Quality Seedlings     

4. Nursery Raising vs Transplanting
These two terms are closely related but are not the same.
Nursery raising
Transplanting
Produces young plants
Moves young plants
Occurs before field planting
Occurs after nursery production
Focuses on seedling development
Focuses on field establishment
Includes germination and nursery care Includes lifting, transport and planting
Produces planting material
Uses planting material

Memory
Nursery Raising = Make the Seedling  
Transplanting = Move the Seedling  

5. Basic Nursery-Raising Workflow
A complete nursery operation can be represented as:
Plan Nursery   
↓
Select Site/Structure   
↓
Select Quality Seed   
↓
Prepare Growing Medium/Bed   
↓
Treat/Prime Seed if Appropriate   
↓
Sow Seed   
↓
Germination   
↓
Seedling Management   

↓
Weed/Pest/Disease Management   
↓
Nutrition + Irrigation   
↓
Hardening   
↓
Seedling Selection   
↓
Transplanting   

6. Types of Nurseries
Nurseries can be classified in several ways.

A. Open-field nursery
Seedlings are raised in beds under relatively natural environmental conditions.
Suitable when:
- climate is reasonably favorable,
- crop tolerates nursery conditions,
- sophisticated protection is not required.

B. Protected nursery
Seedlings are raised under structures that modify the environment.
Examples:
- greenhouse,
- polyhouse,
- shade house,
- net house,
- insect-proof structure.
These can provide better control over:

- temperature,
- radiation,
- wind,
- humidity,
- insects.

C. Container nursery
Seedlings are raised in individual containers or cells.
Examples:
- seedling trays,
- plug trays,
- pots,
- polybags,
- containers.
Advantages include easier:
- handling,
- transportation,
- transplanting,
- root-zone management.

7. Nursery Bed
A nursery bed is a prepared area in which seedlings are raised.
A good nursery bed should provide:
- adequate drainage,
- suitable moisture retention,
- good aeration,
- suitable soil structure,
- easy irrigation,
- good access,

- protection from flooding.
A poorly drained nursery can create serious seedling disease problems.

8. Nursery Site Selection
A suitable nursery site should generally have:

1. Good drainage
Avoid locations where water accumulates.

2. Reliable water supply
Seedlings require frequent and careful water management.

3. Suitable sunlight
Adequate light is necessary for healthy photosynthesis.

## 4. Protection
Protection from:
- strong winds,
- animals,
- flooding,
- excessive heat,
- severe cold.

## 5. Accessibility
The nursery should be convenient for:
- monitoring,
- irrigation,
- fertilization,
- pest management,
- seedling transport.

6. Clean environment
Avoid contamination from diseased plants, contaminated media or unmanaged waste.

9. Nursery Growing Medium
The growing medium provides:
- physical support,
- water,
- oxygen,
- nutrients,
- a suitable root environment.
A good medium balances:
Water Retention+Drainage+Aeration+Root Support  +  
+   +    
Too much water:
Waterlogging→O2↓→Root Stress  O 2  Root\
Stress
Too little water:
Water↓→Wilting→Growth↓   Wilting Growth 10. Properties of a Good Nursery Medium
A suitable medium generally has:
- good water-holding capacity,
- good drainage,
- adequate aeration,
- appropriate physical structure,
- suitable pH,
- adequate nutrients,
- low salinity,
- low contamination,
- low weed-seed load,
- low pathogen risk.
The exact medium depends on crop and nursery system.

11. Soil-Based Nursery
Traditional nurseries may use prepared soil beds.
Advantages:
- simple,
- locally available,
- inexpensive in some systems.
Potential problems:
- soil-borne pathogens,
- weed seeds,
- variable structure,
- compaction,
- uneven fertility.

12. Soilless Nursery Medium
Modern nurseries may use materials such as:
- cocopeat/coir-based materials,
- perlite,
- vermiculite,
- peat-based materials where used,
- composted materials,
- mixtures of different substrates.
The objective is to obtain appropriate:
Water+Air+Support+Root Environment 

13. Seed Selection for Nursery Raising
Nursery quality begins with seed quality.
Good seed should have:
- high viability,

- good germination,
- high vigor,
- genetic purity,
- physical purity,
- good seed health.
Recall:
Seed Quality=Genetic+Physical+Physiological+Health 
Poor-quality seed cannot normally be converted into high-quality seedlings merely through good
nursery management.

14. Seed Treatment
Seed treatment may be used where appropriate to improve:
- seed health,
- protection against certain seed-borne/soil-borne pathogens,
- early establishment.
Depending on crop and production system, treatment may be:
- biological,
- chemical,
- physical,
- nutritional/priming-based.
Treatment should be crop-specific and follow applicable recommendations.

15. Seed Sowing in the Nursery
Important parameters include:
- sowing date,
- seed rate,
- sowing depth,
- spacing,

- seed distribution,
- seed-soil/media contact.
The objective is:
Uniform Germination+Uniform Seedling Development  +
   

16. Nursery Sowing Depth
Seeds should generally be placed at a depth suitable for the crop and seed size.
Too shallow:
- drying,
- poor anchorage,
- exposure.
Too deep:
- delayed emergence,
- weak seedlings,
- emergence failure.
Thus:
Correct Depth→Adequate Moisture+Successful Emergence   +    
There is no single universal nursery sowing depth for all crops.

17. Germination in Nursery
The germination sequence is:
Imbibition→Metabolic Activation→Reserve Mobilization→Radicle Emergence→Seedling       
Water activates metabolism and reserve mobilization.
Oxygen supports respiration.
Suitable temperature supports biochemical activity.

18. Nursery Germination Requirements
The major requirements are:

Water
Required for imbibition and metabolic activation.

Oxygen
Required for respiration.

Temperature
Must fall within a suitable range for the crop.

Seed viability
The embryo must be alive.

Appropriate depth
Must permit successful emergence.

Seed health
Pathogens can destroy seeds or young seedlings.

19. Germination Percentage
A basic measurement is:
Germination%=Normal seedlingsTotal seeds tested×100   Total\ seeds\ tested   100  
Example:
If 95 out of 100 seeds produce normal seedlings:
Germination=95%Germination=95\%

20. Seedling Emergence
Germination and emergence are not exactly the same.

Germination
The seed resumes growth and produces the radicle/seedling structures.

Emergence
The developing shoot becomes visible above the soil or growing medium.
Therefore:
Germination≠Emergence 

21. Water Management in Nursery
Water management is one of the most important nursery operations.

Underwatering
Can cause:
- wilting,
- slow growth,
- poor germination,
- seedling mortality.

Overwatering
Can cause:
- poor aeration,
- root stress,
- damping-off,
- nutrient leaching,
- weak root systems.
Therefore:
Nursery Irrigation=Adequate Moisture without Waterlogging  =
   

22. Irrigation Methods
Depending on nursery design, water may be applied through:
- watering cans,
- hoses,
- sprinklers,

- micro-sprinklers,
- drip systems,
- automated misting systems.
Very fine seeds and young seedlings may require gentle application to avoid:
- seed displacement,
- soil erosion,
- root exposure.

23. Light Management
Light is essential for photosynthesis after seedlings become photosynthetically active.
Insufficient light can cause:
- elongated stems,
- weak seedlings,
- pale leaves,
- poor biomass accumulation.
Excessive radiation can cause:
- high leaf temperature,
- water stress,
- leaf injury in sensitive seedlings.
Thus:
Light Management=Adequate Light without Excess Stress   

24. Temperature Management
Temperature affects:
- germination,
- root growth,
- shoot growth,
- enzyme activity,

- photosynthesis,
- respiration.
Extreme temperatures can reduce seedling quality.
Protected nurseries can modify temperature through:
- ventilation,
- shading,
- heating,
- cooling,
- structure design.

25. Humidity Management
Humidity affects water loss.
High humidity:
- reduces atmospheric drying demand,
- but excessive humidity can increase disease/condensation risk.
Low humidity:
- increases water loss,
- can increase wilting.
The relevant atmospheric concept is:
VPD=es−ea 
High VPD generally means greater atmospheric demand for water.

## 26. Ventilation
Ventilation is important in protected nurseries.
It helps manage:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- condensation,
- gas exchange.

Poor ventilation can create a warm, humid environment favorable to some diseases.

27. Nursery Nutrition
Seedlings require nutrients for:
- root development,
- leaf development,
- photosynthesis,
- enzyme activity,
- structural growth.
Major nutrients include:
- N,
- P,
- K.
Secondary and micronutrients may also be important depending on the crop and growing medium.

28. Nitrogen in Nursery
Nitrogen supports:
- chlorophyll,
- proteins,
- enzymes,
- vegetative growth.
Deficiency can cause:
- pale leaves,
- reduced growth.
Excess can produce:
- overly soft/lush growth,
- weak seedlings,
- greater susceptibility to some stresses/problems.
Therefore:

More Nitrogen≠Better Seedling 

29. Phosphorus in Nursery
Phosphorus is important for:
- energy metabolism,
- root development,
- early establishment.
Adequate P can support healthy root development, but excessive application is not automatically
beneficial.

30. Potassium in Nursery
Potassium supports:
- water regulation,
- enzyme activation,
- stomatal function,
- stress responses,
- structural and physiological performance.
Balanced nutrition is more important than maximizing any single nutrient.

31. Weed Management
Weeds compete with seedlings for:
- water,
- nutrients,
- light,
- space.
Because seedlings are small, competition can be significant.
Possible approaches:
- clean growing media,
- sanitation,

- hand weeding,
- mulching where appropriate,
- suitable mechanical methods,
- approved chemical methods where appropriate.

32. Pest Management
Nursery seedlings can be attacked by:
- sucking insects,
- chewing insects,
- soil insects,
- mites,
- other pests.
Regular monitoring is important because a small nursery pest population can spread rapidly when
plants are densely packed.

33. Disease Management
Nursery disease can be especially destructive because seedlings are closely spaced.
Potential problems include:
- damping-off,
- root diseases,
- leaf diseases,
- seed-borne infections.
Risk increases under conditions such as:
Excess Moisture+Poor Aeration+High Humidity+Pathogen 
Good sanitation and appropriate water management are important.

## 34. Damping-Off
Damping-off is a common nursery disease problem in which young seedlings are infected near the
soil/media surface and may collapse or die.
A typical sequence is:
Pathogen+Susceptible Seedling+Favorable Moisture/Environment→Seedling Disease 
+   +    Management depends on the causal organism and crop, but commonly emphasizes:
- clean seed,
- clean media,
- proper drainage,
- avoiding excessive irrigation,
- adequate spacing,
- sanitation,
- appropriate crop-specific disease management.

35. Nursery Sanitation
Sanitation means maintaining a clean production environment.
Important practices include:
- clean trays,
- clean tools,
- clean water where feasible,
- healthy seed,
- clean growing medium,
- removal of diseased seedlings,
- proper disposal of infected material,
- avoiding unnecessary movement of contaminated material.
A nursery can become a source of disease for the entire field.
Infected Nursery→Infected Transplants→Field Disease Risk 

36. Seedling Spacing
Seedlings need adequate space for:
- light interception,
- air movement,
- root growth,
- shoot growth.
Overcrowding can cause:
- competition,
- weak/elongated seedlings,
- poor ventilation,
- greater humidity,
- disease risk.

37. Seedling Thinning
If too many seedlings emerge in one location, excess seedlings may be removed.
Purpose:
- reduce competition,
- improve spacing,
- produce stronger seedlings.
Thus:
Thinning→Competition↓→Individual Seedling Resources↑ 

38. Seedling Pricking/Transplanting Within
Nursery
In some nursery systems, very young seedlings may first be germinated densely and later moved into
individual cells or wider spacing.
This can be called pricking out or nursery-stage transplanting.

Therefore there can be:
Seed→Germination Bed→Individual Container→Field 
This is different from the final field transplanting operation.

39. Root Development
A good nursery should produce a balanced root system.
Important characteristics:
- sufficient root length,
- lateral root development,
- healthy root color/structure,
- good root branching,
- absence of severe root disease.
A healthy root system improves the plant's ability to recover after transplanting.

40. Root-to-Shoot Ratio
A useful conceptual measure is:
R:S=Root BiomassShoot Biomass   Shoot\ Biomass   
A seedling should have a balanced root and shoot system rather than excessive shoot growth with
inadequate roots.

41. Seedling Vigor
Seedling vigor refers to the ability of a seedling to grow rapidly and uniformly and withstand
environmental stresses during establishment.
Vigorous seedlings generally show:
- strong stems,
- healthy leaves,
- active roots,
- uniform growth,

- good recovery after handling.
Vigor is not identical to germination percentage.
Germination≠Vigor 

42. Seedling Hardening
Before transplanting, seedlings may need to be hardened.
Hardening = gradual acclimatization to field conditions.
The nursery environment may have:
- less wind,
- higher humidity,
- lower radiation,
- more controlled temperature.
The field may have:
- stronger wind,
- greater radiation,
- greater VPD,
- wider temperature fluctuations.
Thus:
Hardening→Gradual Environmental Adjustment 

43. Why Hardening Is Important
Without adequate acclimatization, seedlings may experience:
- severe wilting,
- leaf scorch,
- growth reduction,
- transplant shock,
- delayed recovery.
Hardening should be crop- and environment-specific rather than an arbitrary reduction of irrigation or
shade.

44. Seedling Selection Before Transplanting
Select seedlings that are:
- healthy,
- vigorous,
- uniform,
- true-to-type,
- properly developed,
- appropriately sized,
- adequately rooted.
Avoid seedlings that are:
- severely diseased,
- badly damaged,
- severely weak,
- severely root-bound,
- off-type.

45. Transport of Nursery Seedlings
If seedlings must be transported to the field, care is needed to avoid:
- drying,
- mechanical injury,
- root exposure,
- overheating,
- excessive compression.
The shorter and gentler the handling chain, the easier it is for seedlings to recover.

46. Nursery Seedling Age
There is no universal ideal age.
It depends on:

- crop,
- variety/hybrid,
- seedling growth rate,
- season,
- nursery conditions,
- root development,
- transplanting method.
The general principle is:
Appropriate Age=Adequate Development+High Recovery Potential  =
  +    

47. Very Young vs Over-Aged Seedlings
Very young
Over-aged
Weak root system
Root binding possible
Small shoot
Excessive shoot growth
Fragile
May become hardened/less flexible in some systems
Greater handling sensitivity Greater transplant shock risk
Insufficient development
Delayed field recovery possible
The target is an appropriately developed seedling, not simply the youngest or oldest.

48. Container Seedlings
Container seedlings are commonly raised in:
- plug trays,
- pots,
- bags.
Advantages:
- controlled root volume,
- easier handling,
- reduced root disturbance,
- uniform spacing,
- efficient nursery management.

Potential problem:
Excessive Time in Container→Root Binding 

49. Plug Seedlings
A plug is a small root-and-growing-medium unit containing a seedling.
A good plug generally has:
- cohesive root-medium structure,
- healthy roots,
- adequate root development,
- minimal root damage during removal.
Plug systems are widely useful for mechanized or precise transplanting.

50. Bare-Root Seedlings
In a bare-root system, seedlings are removed with most of the growing medium removed from their
roots.
Advantages:
- lighter,
- potentially easier to transport in some systems.
Risks:
- root drying,
- root damage,
- greater transplant shock.
Therefore bare-root plants require careful handling.

51. Nursery Raising and Water Use
Nursery production can make water management more targeted because a relatively small area is used
to produce seedlings.
Conceptually:

Small Nursery Area→Focused Resource Management 
The seedlings are later transferred to the larger production field.
However, nursery production still requires water and infrastructure.

52. Nursery Raising and Seed Requirement
Because seedlings are raised under managed conditions, nursery systems can potentially use seed
efficiently.
The exact seed requirement depends on:
- germination,
- expected nursery losses,
- thinning,
- transplant survival,
- desired final population.
A conceptual relationship is:
Required Seed=Desired TransplantsGermination×Nursery Survival   Germination Nursery\ Survival   
where percentages are expressed as fractions.

53. Nursery Losses
Seedling losses can occur due to:
- poor germination,
- damping-off,
- drought,
- excessive water,
- nutrient imbalance,
- pests,
- temperature stress,
- handling damage.

Therefore nursery planning should include some allowance for expected losses rather than assuming
every seed becomes a transplantable plant.

54. Nursery Raising and Transplant Survival
The quality of nursery seedlings affects field establishment.
Conceptually:
Seed Quality→Seedling Quality→Transplant Quality→Field Establishment 
Poor nursery management can therefore create field problems before the crop even reaches the field.

55. Nursery Raising and Direct Sowing
Nursery route
Seed→Nursery→Seedling→Transplant→Field 

Direct-sowing route
Seed→Field→Germination→Seedling→Crop 
The key difference is where the early seedling stage occurs.

56. Advantages of Nursery Raising
1. Better early-stage management
Environmental conditions can be managed more carefully.

2. Efficient seed use
Especially useful for expensive seed.

3. Seedling selection
Weak or diseased seedlings can be removed before field planting.

4. Uniform planting material
Seedlings can be selected for similar developmental stage.

## 5. Protection
Young plants can receive protection from harsh environmental conditions.

6. Better use of field preparation time
The nursery and field can be prepared simultaneously.

7. Efficient use of protected structures
High-value crops can receive controlled early growth.

57. Disadvantages of Nursery Raising
1. Additional cost
Requires nursery materials and management.

2. Additional labor
Seedlings must be managed and later transported/planted.

3. Transplant shock
The seedlings experience environmental and root disturbance.

4. Disease concentration
Dense nursery populations can allow diseases to spread rapidly.

5. Nursery infrastructure
May require:
- trays,
- structures,
- growing medium,
- irrigation systems.

6. Timing constraints
Seedlings must be transplanted at an appropriate stage.

58. Nursery Raising and Mechanization
Modern nurseries can be highly mechanized.
Operations may include:

- substrate preparation,
- tray filling,
- seed sowing,
- irrigation,
- fertilization,
- environmental control,
- seedling handling,
- transplanting.
Automation can improve uniformity but increases capital and technical requirements.

59. Nursery Raising in Protected Cultivation
Protected nursery systems can provide greater control over:
- temperature,
- humidity,
- radiation,
- pests,
- wind,
- water.
A simplified system is:
Protected Nursery→High-quality Seedling→Protected/Open Field Production quality\ Seedling  Protected/Open\ Field\ Production  

60. Nursery Raising for Grafted Plants
Some horticultural crops are raised as rootstock seedlings and later grafted.
The sequence can be:
Seed→Rootstock Seedling→Grafting→Grafted Plant→Nursery Growth→Field 
Thus nursery raising can be part of grafted-plant production.

61. Nursery Raising and Rootstocks
A nursery seedling may be produced specifically to become a rootstock.
For example:
Seed→Rootstock Seedling→Graft/Bud→Composite Plant 
Therefore:
Every rootstock seedling is a seedling, but not every nursery seedling is a rootstock.
   

62. Nursery Raising and Seedling Health
The nursery is the first major health checkpoint.
A healthy nursery should minimize:
Seed−borne pathogens+Soil/media−borne pathogens+Insect pests+Weeds 
Healthy nursery material reduces the probability of introducing serious problems into the production
field.

63. Nursery Monitoring
Regular monitoring should examine:

Germination
Are seeds emerging uniformly?

Growth
Are seedlings developing normally?

Roots
Are roots healthy?

Leaves
Are there symptoms of nutrient deficiency or disease?

Pests
Are insects or mites present?

Moisture
Is the medium too wet or too dry?

Temperature
Are seedlings experiencing heat/cold stress?

Uniformity
Are seedlings at similar developmental stages?

64. Important Nursery Measurements
Useful measurements can include:

Germination percentage
G%=Normal seedlingsSeeds tested×100G\%=   Seeds\ tested  100

Survival percentage
Survival%=Living seedlingsSeedlings established×100Survival\%=  
 Seedlings\ established  100

Seedling height
Measures shoot development.

Root length
Indicates root development.

Seedling dry weight
Useful for assessing biomass.

Root-to-shoot ratio
R:S=Root biomassShoot biomassR:S=   Shoot\ biomass 

Uniformity
Measures consistency of seedling development.

65. Nursery Quality → Field Establishment
The relationship can be expressed as:
Seed Quality   

↓
Germination   
↓
Seedling Vigor   
↓
Root Development   
↓
Hardening   
↓
Transplanting   
↓
Root Recovery   
↓
Field Establishment   
↓
Growth and Yield   

66. Common Nursery-Raising Problems
Problem
Poor germination
Delayed emergence
Seedling wilting
Root rot
Damping-off
Yellow seedlings
Tall/weak seedlings
Uneven seedlings
Root binding
Seed displacement
Leaf scorch
High mortality after
transplanting

Possible causes
Poor seed, unsuitable temperature/moisture, dormancy
Deep sowing, low temperature, poor vigor
Low moisture, high VPD, root damage
Excess moisture, poor drainage, pathogens
Pathogen + favorable moisture/environment

Low light, overcrowding, excessive N
Uneven water, light, seed depth or seed quality

67. Common Nursery-Raising Mistakes
1. Using poor-quality seed
This limits seedling quality from the beginning.

2. Excessive irrigation
Creates poor aeration and disease conditions.

3. Insufficient irrigation
Causes water stress and mortality.

4. Excessive fertilizer
Can cause salt injury and overly soft growth.

## 5. Overcrowding
Creates competition and poor airflow.

6. Poor sanitation
Allows disease spread.

7. Using contaminated growing media
Can introduce pathogens and weeds.

8. Keeping seedlings too long
Can produce root binding and over-aged seedlings.

9. Poor hardening
Increases transplant shock.

10. Rough handling
Damages roots and shoots.

68. Nursery Raising and Climate
Nursery conditions should be matched to the crop's requirements.
Important environmental factors:
Temperature+Light+Water+Humidity+Air movement+Growing medium 
A nursery can therefore act as a microenvironment-management system.

69. Nursery Raising and Microclimate
Inside a protected nursery, the farmer can modify the seedling microclimate.
For example:
Shade→Radiation↓  Radiation Ventilation→Humidity/Temperature Management  Humidity/Temperature\
Management Irrigation→Root−zone Moisture Management  Root-zone\
Moisture\ Management
This can improve seedling conditions when properly managed.

70. Nursery Raising and Hardening — Full
Transition
The plant experiences:

Nursery
Controlled Environment 
↓

Hardening
Gradual Environmental Exposure 
↓

Field
Natural/Production Environment 
Therefore:
Nursery→Hardening→Field 
is an important transition pathway.

71. Nursery Raising and Final Transplanting
When seedlings reach the appropriate stage:
1. irrigation/handling is managed appropriately,
2. healthy seedlings are selected,

3. seedlings are lifted or removed from containers,
4. roots are protected,
5. seedlings are transported,
6. field planting positions are prepared,
7. seedlings are planted,
8. root-zone soil contact is established,
9. moisture is managed,
10.seedlings are monitored for recovery.

72. Nursery Raising vs Direct Sowing —
Complete Comparison

73. Nursery Raising vs Seedling
These terms also differ.

Nursery raising
The process of producing young plants.

Seedling
The young plant produced.
Nursery Raising=Process =   
Seedling=Product of that process =   

74. Nursery Raising vs Planting Material
Planting material is broader.
It can include:
- seeds,
- seedlings,
- cuttings,
- tubers,
- bulbs,
- rhizomes,
- suckers,
- grafted plants.
Nursery raising primarily produces young plants/seedlings, although nurseries can also produce
grafted or other vegetatively propagated plants.

75. Nursery Raising vs Protected Cultivation
They are not the same.

Nursery raising
A method of producing young plants.

Protected cultivation
A production system in which environmental conditions are modified/protected using structures or
technologies.
A nursery can be:
- open,
- protected,
- container-based.
Therefore:
Protected Nursery⊆Nursery Raising 
but nursery raising does not require protected cultivation.

76. Nursery Raising and Economics
Nursery raising introduces additional costs:
- seed,
- trays,
- growing medium,
- water,
- fertilizer,
- structures,
- labor,
- pest/disease management,
- transportation.
But it can provide economic benefits through:
- efficient seed use,
- improved seedling quality,
- reduced field establishment risk,
- uniformity,
- better use of land and time.
The economic result depends on the crop and production system.

77. Ideal Nursery Seedling
An ideal transplantable seedling generally has:
Healthy Roots+Strong Stem+Healthy Leaves+Good Vigor+Uniformity+True-to-Type+Good Health xed    +   +   +   +
  +   +    
It should be neither excessively young nor excessively old.

where germination and survival are expressed as fractions.

80. Important Distinctions to Remember
Term

Seed

81. Most Important Conceptual Difference
Your previous topics can now be connected perfectly:
Seed   

Route 1 — Direct sowing
Seed→Final Field→Germination→Seedling→Crop       

Route 2 — Nursery raising
Seed→Nursery→Germination→Seedling→Hardening→Transplanting→Field 
       
This is the central relationship.

82. Final Conceptual Model
Nursery raising is essentially a controlled seed-to-seedling production system.

Quality Seed
